========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Hi, yeah—thanks for seeing me. I came in because I’m having a really hard time staying with things that I genuinely intend to do. Like, I’m a secondary school teacher, and I’ll sit down with a stack of essays to mark or some curriculum document I need to read, and I’ll be completely sure I’m about to get through it properly. I’ll make the tea, clear the desk, open the first paper, all of that.

And then, honestly, within maybe ten minutes, I realize I’ve just been staring at the same page. My eyes have moved over it, but I haven’t actually taken in what the student wrote, or I’ve read the same paragraph three times and still couldn’t tell you what it said. Meanwhile my mind has gone off in three different directions—something about tomorrow’s lesson, some unrelated thing I need to do, some random thought—and then I sort of snap back and feel frustrated because I was technically “working,” but nothing has really happened. That’s the main reason I’m here.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, I think so. I mean, I didn’t have the words for it then, obviously, and I wasn’t a disruptive child or anything, so it sort of got framed differently. But even in primary school my reports would say things like, “bright but careless,” which I remember because my parents used to repeat it to me constantly, like it was this little phrase that explained everything.

And there were comments about me not finishing work unless the teacher was really keeping an eye on me. I’d understand the lesson, or at least seem to, but then the actual worksheet or written task just wouldn’t get completed unless someone was practically hovering. My parents were always finding crumpled worksheets in my backpack as well—things I’d done partly, or even finished sometimes, but just forgot to turn in. They’d be all folded up at the bottom of my bag days later, and I’d feel embarrassed because I hadn’t meant to not hand them in. It wasn’t like I was refusing; it was more like it just slipped out of my awareness completely.

I mean, I didn’t have the words for it then, obviously, and I wasn’t a disruptive child or anything, so it sort of got framed differently. But even in primary school my reports would say things like, “bright but careless,” which I remember because my parents used to repeat it to me constantly, like it was this little phrase that explained everything.\n\nAnd there were comments about me not finishing work unless the teacher was really keeping an eye on me. I’d understand the lesson, or at least seem to, but then the actual worksheet or written task just wouldn’t get completed unless someone was practically hovering. Um… I’m not sure, actually. I mean, I do worry about things in the ordinary sense, like if I’ve missed something or if I’m behind on work, but I don’t know that I’d describe it as spending a lot of time worrying in a way I can’t control.

I don’t think so, no—not long periods in that sense. I mean, I do keep lists and I use a calendar, especially because teaching sort of forces you to have some structure, otherwise the day just falls apart. So from the outside I probably look reasonably organized in certain ways.

But if you mean sitting down with a bigger piece of work and naturally knowing, right, first I do this, then this, then this, and keeping all those moving parts straight… I’m not sure that comes very easily to me. I often feel like I have to externalize everything—write it down, make little sub-lists, put reminders in different places—otherwise it gets a bit vague and I lose track of what I was meant to be doing next.

Though I don’t know if that’s unusual or just how everyone manages things. I’ve sort of built a lot of habits around it, so it’s hard to tell what I’m like without those systems.
=====================================

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I think it does, actually. I’d like to say it’s mainly a work thing because teaching is just hectic and there are so many little bits to keep track of, but it’s not only there.

At work it’s become a bit more serious than just me feeling disorganized. My department chair has put me on a support plan because of missed follow-through—things I said I’d do, forms or emails or follow-ups that didn’t happen when they were supposed to. That was pretty embarrassing, honestly, because I don’t feel like I don’t care. I do care. But things slip, or I think I’ll come back to them and then I don’t, and by the time someone asks, it looks like I’ve just ignored it.

And then at home, my wife has started taking over a lot of the bills and reminders. Not because we agreed that was just her role or anything, but because she can’t really count on me to keep track of them consistently. I’ll mean to pay something or remember something, and then it’s either late or she’s had to remind me several times, so she’s started just doing it herself. Which makes me feel guilty, because it’s not fair on her.

So yes, it’s definitely more than one area. It’s work, but it’s also home stuff, and I think that’s part of why I’m here.

At least, that’s how I’d describe it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble organizing tasks, like breaking work into steps or keeping track of what you need to do?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that, because it depends what counts as “organizing” in the way you mean. I mean, I do keep lists and I use a calendar, especially because teaching sort of forces you to have some structure, otherwise the day just falls apart. So from the outside I probably look reasonably organized in certain ways.\n\nBut if you mean sitting down with a bigger piece of work and naturally knowing, right, first I do this, then this, then this, and keeping all those moving parts straight… I’m not sure that comes very easily to me. I often feel like I have to externalize everything—write it down, make little sub-lists, put reminders in different places—otherwise it gets a bit vague and I lose track of what I was meant to be doing next.\n\nThough I don’t know if that’s unusual or just how everyone manages things. I’ve sort of built a lot of habits around it, so it’s hard to tell what I’m like without those systems.\n\nDoctor: Do these difficulties with focus and follow-through cause problems in more than one area of your life, not just at work?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it does, actually. I’d like to say it’s mainly a work thing because teaching is just hectic and there are so many little bits to keep track of, but it’s not only there.\n\nAt work it’s become a bit more serious than just me feeling disorganized. My department chair has put me on a support plan because of missed follow-through—things I said I’d do, forms or emails or follow-ups that didn’t happen when they were supposed to. That was pretty embarrassing, honestly, because I don’t feel like I don’t care. I do care. But things slip, or I think I’ll come back to them and then I don’t, and by the time someone asks, it looks like I’ve just ignored it.\n\nAnd then at home, my wife has started taking over a lot of the bills and reminders. Like, I’m a secondary school teacher, and I’ll sit down with a stack of essays to mark or some curriculum document I need to read, and I’ll be completely sure I’m about to get through it properly. I’ll make the tea, clear the desk, open the first paper, all of that.\n\nAnd then, honestly, within maybe ten minutes, I realize I’ve just been staring at the same page. I’m not sure, actually. I wouldn’t say that’s a big thing I’ve noticed about myself. I mean, everyone says the wrong thing now and then, or jumps in a bit too quickly in a conversation, but I don’t think people would describe me as someone who’s constantly blurting things out or acting without any filter.

If anything, I probably spend quite a lot of time thinking about what I’m going to say, especially at work, because as a teacher you sort of have to be careful with your words. I can occasionally interrupt if I’m anxious to get a point in before I forget it, but I don’t know if that’s the same as acting before thinking. It doesn’t feel like I’m impulsive in that way, if that’s what you mean.

So… no, not often, at least not that I’ve noticed.

I often feel like I have to externalize everything—write it down, make little sub-lists, put reminders in different places—otherwise it gets a bit vague and I lose track of what I was meant to be doing next.\n\nThough I don’t know if that’s unusual or just how everyone manages things. I’ve sort of built a lot of habits around it, so it’s hard to tell what I’m like without those systems.\n\nDoctor: Do these difficulties with focus and follow-through cause problems in more than one area of your life, not just at work?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it does, actually. I’d like to say it’s mainly a work thing because teaching is just hectic and there are so many little bits to keep track of, but it’s not only there.\n\nAt work it’s become a bit more serious than just me feeling disorganized. My department chair has put me on a support plan because of missed follow-through—things I said I’d do, forms or emails or follow-ups that didn’t happen when they were supposed to. That was pretty embarrassing, honestly, because I don’t feel like I don’t care. I do care. But things slip, or I think I’ll come back to them and then I don’t, and by the time someone asks, it looks like I’ve just ignored it.\n\nAnd then at home, my wife has started taking over a lot of the bills and reminders. Not because we agreed that was just her role or anything, but because she can’t really count on me to keep track of them consistently. I’ll mean to pay something or remember something, and then it’s either late or she’s had to remind me several times, so she’s started just doing it herself. Which makes me feel guilty, because it’s not fair on her.\n\nSo yes, it’s definitely more than one area. It’s work, but it’s also home stuff, and I think that’s part of why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act or speak before thinking, even when you mean not to?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, actually. I wouldn’t say that’s a big thing I’ve noticed about myself. I mean, everyone says the wrong thing now and then, or jumps in a bit too quickly in a conversation, but I don’t think people would describe me as someone who’s constantly blurting things out or acting without any filter.\n\nIf anything, I probably spend quite a lot of time thinking about what I’m going to say, especially at work, because as a teacher you sort of have to be careful with your words. I can occasionally interrupt if I’m anxious to get a point in before I forget it, but I don’t know if that’s the same as acting before thinking. It doesn’t feel like I’m impulsive in that way, if that’s what you mean.\n\nSo… no, not often, at least not that I’ve noticed.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I don’t think so, not in a really obvious way. I mean, I’m not someone who’s constantly pacing around or unable to stay in a chair, if that’s what you mean.

I can sit through meetings, and I can sit at a desk if I need to, although I suppose I might shift around or tap a pen or change position without really noticing. But I wouldn’t say I feel driven to move all the time. It’s more that sitting still can feel a bit uncomfortable if I’m bored or waiting for something, but I’ve always assumed that’s pretty normal.

So… I don’t know. I don’t think physical restlessness is one of the main things I’d point to. At least not compared with other things.

But if you mean constant worry that sort of takes over and I can’t switch off from it, I’m not sure that’s really the main thing for me.\n\nDoctor: Have there been long periods when you felt persistently down or unable to enjoy things?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no—not long periods in that sense. I mean, I have definitely had times where I’ve felt fed up or discouraged, especially when I’m not keeping up with things the way I feel I should be, but I wouldn’t say I’ve had stretches where everything feels persistently bleak or where I can’t enjoy anything at all.\n\nLike, I can still enjoy things when I actually get to them—seeing friends, watching something, having a good lesson at school, that sort of thing. My mood can dip when I feel like I’ve let things pile up, but it feels more tied to the situation rather than this continuous low mood that takes over for weeks or months. At least, that’s how I’d describe it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble organizing tasks, like breaking work into steps or keeping track of what you need to do?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that, because it depends what counts as “organizing” in the way you mean. I mean, I do keep lists and I use a calendar, especially because teaching sort of forces you to have some structure, otherwise the day just falls apart. So from the outside I probably look reasonably organized in certain ways.\n\nBut if you mean sitting down with a bigger piece of work and naturally knowing, right, first I do this, then this, then this, and keeping all those moving parts straight… I’m not sure that comes very easily to me. I often feel like I have to externalize everything—write it down, make little sub-lists, put reminders in different places—otherwise it gets a bit vague and I lose track of what I was meant to be doing next.\n\nThough I don’t know if that’s unusual or just how everyone manages things. I’ve sort of built a lot of habits around it, so it’s hard to tell what I’m like without those systems.\n\nDoctor: Do these difficulties with focus and follow-through cause problems in more than one area of your life, not just at work?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it does, actually. I’d like to say it’s mainly a work thing because teaching is just hectic and there are so many little bits to keep track of, but it’s not only there.\n\nAt work it’s become a bit more serious than just me feeling disorganized. My department chair has put me on a support plan because of missed follow-through—things I said I’d do, forms or emails or follow-ups that didn’t happen when they were supposed to. That was pretty embarrassing, honestly, because I don’t feel like I don’t care. I do care. But things slip, or I think I’ll come back to them and then I don’t, and by the time someone asks, it looks like I’ve just ignored it.\n\nAnd then at home, my wife has started taking over a lot of the bills and reminders. Not because we agreed that was just her role or anything, but because she can’t really count on me to keep track of them consistently. I’ll mean to pay something or remember something, and then it’s either late or she’s had to remind me several times, so she’s started just doing it herself. Which makes me feel guilty, because it’s not fair on her.\n\nSo yes, it’s definitely more than one area. It’s work, but it’s also home stuff, and I think that’s part of why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act or speak before thinking, even when you mean not to?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, actually. I wouldn’t say that’s a big thing I’ve noticed about myself. I mean, everyone says the wrong thing now and then, or jumps in a bit too quickly in a conversation, but I don’t think people would describe me as someone who’s constantly blurting things out or acting without any filter.\n\nIf anything, I probably spend quite a lot of time thinking about what I’m going to say, especially at work, because as a teacher you sort of have to be careful with your words. I can occasionally interrupt if I’m anxious to get a point in before I forget it, but I don’t know if that’s the same as acting before thinking. It doesn’t feel like I’m impulsive in that way, if that’s what you mean.\n\nSo… no, not often, at least not that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel physically restless, like it’s hard to sit still?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in a really obvious way. I mean, I’m not someone who’s constantly pacing around or unable to stay in a chair, if that’s what you mean.\n\nI can sit through meetings, and I can sit at a desk if I need to, although I suppose I might shift around or tap a pen or change position without really noticing. But I wouldn’t say I feel driven to move all the time. It’s more that sitting still can feel a bit uncomfortable if I’m bored or waiting for something, but I’ve always assumed that’s pretty normal.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I don’t think physical restlessness is one of the main things I’d point to. At least not compared with other things.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, definitely. I mean, that’s part of what makes it confusing for me, because it’s not like I can never focus on anything. If something genuinely catches my interest, I can stay with it much more easily, sometimes to the point where I lose track of time a bit.

But with things that are important but not immediately engaging, it’s completely different. A big reason I came in is because I can sit down with a stack of essays or a curriculum document, fully intending to get through it, and then ten minutes later I realize I’ve just been staring at the same page. I’ll have read the same paragraph several times, but I’m thinking about three other things at once and barely taking in what’s actually in front of me.

And it’s frustrating because from the outside it probably looks like I’m being lazy or avoiding it, but internally I’m trying. I’ve sat down, I’ve opened the document, I’ve got the pen in my hand, and somehow my attention just… slides off it. So yes, if it’s interesting, it’s much easier, but if it isn’t, even when it matters, staying focused can feel weirdly hard.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n9\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F41.9, F32.9, F48.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Hi, yeah—thanks for seeing me. I came in because I’m having a really hard time staying with things that I genuinely intend to do. Like, I’m a secondary school teacher, and I’ll sit down with a stack of essays to mark or some curriculum document I need to read, and I’ll be completely sure I’m about to get through it properly. I’ll make the tea, clear the desk, open the first paper, all of that.\n\nAnd then, honestly, within maybe ten minutes, I realize I’ve just been staring at the same page. My eyes have moved over it, but I haven’t actually taken in what the student wrote, or I’ve read the same paragraph three times and still couldn’t tell you what it said. Meanwhile my mind has gone off in three different directions—something about tomorrow’s lesson, some unrelated thing I need to do, some random thought—and then I sort of snap back and feel frustrated because I was technically “working,” but nothing has really happened. That’s the main reason I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Did you have this same kind of difficulty focusing when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. I mean, I didn’t have the words for it then, obviously, and I wasn’t a disruptive child or anything, so it sort of got framed differently. But even in primary school my reports would say things like, “bright but careless,” which I remember because my parents used to repeat it to me constantly, like it was this little phrase that explained everything.\n\nAnd there were comments about me not finishing work unless the teacher was really keeping an eye on me. I’d understand the lesson, or at least seem to, but then the actual worksheet or written task just wouldn’t get completed unless someone was practically hovering. My parents were always finding crumpled worksheets in my backpack as well—things I’d done partly, or even finished sometimes, but just forgot to turn in. They’d be all folded up at the bottom of my bag days later, and I’d feel embarrassed because I hadn’t meant to not hand them in. It wasn’t like I was refusing; it was more like it just slipped out of my awareness completely.\n\nDoctor: Do you spend a lot of time worrying that you can’t control?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not sure, actually. I mean, I do worry about things in the ordinary sense, like if I’ve missed something or if I’m behind on work, but I don’t know that I’d describe it as spending a lot of time worrying in a way I can’t control.\n\nIt’s more that I get frustrated or preoccupied when something hasn’t gone the way I meant it to, if that makes sense. But if you mean constant worry that sort of takes over and I can’t switch off from it, I’m not sure that’s really the main thing for me.\n\nDoctor: Have there been long periods when you felt persistently down or unable to enjoy things?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no—not long periods in that sense. I mean, I have definitely had times where I’ve felt fed up or discouraged, especially when I’m not keeping up with things the way I feel I should be, but I wouldn’t say I’ve had stretches where everything feels persistently bleak or where I can’t enjoy anything at all.\n\nLike, I can still enjoy things when I actually get to them—seeing friends, watching something, having a good lesson at school, that sort of thing. My mood can dip when I feel like I’ve let things pile up, but it feels more tied to the situation rather than this continuous low mood that takes over for weeks or months. At least, that’s how I’d describe it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble organizing tasks, like breaking work into steps or keeping track of what you need to do?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that, because it depends what counts as “organizing” in the way you mean. I mean, I do keep lists and I use a calendar, especially because teaching sort of forces you to have some structure, otherwise the day just falls apart. So from the outside I probably look reasonably organized in certain ways.\n\nBut if you mean sitting down with a bigger piece of work and naturally knowing, right, first I do this, then this, then this, and keeping all those moving parts straight… I’m not sure that comes very easily to me. I often feel like I have to externalize everything—write it down, make little sub-lists, put reminders in different places—otherwise it gets a bit vague and I lose track of what I was meant to be doing next.\n\nThough I don’t know if that’s unusual or just how everyone manages things. I’ve sort of built a lot of habits around it, so it’s hard to tell what I’m like without those systems.\n\nDoctor: Do these difficulties with focus and follow-through cause problems in more than one area of your life, not just at work?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it does, actually. I’d like to say it’s mainly a work thing because teaching is just hectic and there are so many little bits to keep track of, but it’s not only there.\n\nAt work it’s become a bit more serious than just me feeling disorganized. My department chair has put me on a support plan because of missed follow-through—things I said I’d do, forms or emails or follow-ups that didn’t happen when they were supposed to. That was pretty embarrassing, honestly, because I don’t feel like I don’t care. I do care. But things slip, or I think I’ll come back to them and then I don’t, and by the time someone asks, it looks like I’ve just ignored it.\n\nAnd then at home, my wife has started taking over a lot of the bills and reminders. Not because we agreed that was just her role or anything, but because she can’t really count on me to keep track of them consistently. I’ll mean to pay something or remember something, and then it’s either late or she’s had to remind me several times, so she’s started just doing it herself. Which makes me feel guilty, because it’s not fair on her.\n\nSo yes, it’s definitely more than one area. It’s work, but it’s also home stuff, and I think that’s part of why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act or speak before thinking, even when you mean not to?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, actually. I wouldn’t say that’s a big thing I’ve noticed about myself. I mean, everyone says the wrong thing now and then, or jumps in a bit too quickly in a conversation, but I don’t think people would describe me as someone who’s constantly blurting things out or acting without any filter.\n\nIf anything, I probably spend quite a lot of time thinking about what I’m going to say, especially at work, because as a teacher you sort of have to be careful with your words. I can occasionally interrupt if I’m anxious to get a point in before I forget it, but I don’t know if that’s the same as acting before thinking. It doesn’t feel like I’m impulsive in that way, if that’s what you mean.\n\nSo… no, not often, at least not that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel physically restless, like it’s hard to sit still?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in a really obvious way. I mean, I’m not someone who’s constantly pacing around or unable to stay in a chair, if that’s what you mean.\n\nI can sit through meetings, and I can sit at a desk if I need to, although I suppose I might shift around or tap a pen or change position without really noticing. But I wouldn’t say I feel driven to move all the time. It’s more that sitting still can feel a bit uncomfortable if I’m bored or waiting for something, but I’ve always assumed that’s pretty normal.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I don’t think physical restlessness is one of the main things I’d point to. At least not compared with other things.\n\nDoctor: When a task is genuinely interesting to you, is it much easier for you to stay focused on it?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. I mean, that’s part of what makes it confusing for me, because it’s not like I can never focus on anything. If something genuinely catches my interest, I can stay with it much more easily, sometimes to the point where I lose track of time a bit.\n\nBut with things that are important but not immediately engaging, it’s completely different. A big reason I came in is because I can sit down with a stack of essays or a curriculum document, fully intending to get through it, and then ten minutes later I realize I’ve just been staring at the same page. I’ll have read the same paragraph several times, but I’m thinking about three other things at once and barely taking in what’s actually in front of me.\n\nAnd it’s frustrating because from the outside it probably looks like I’m being lazy or avoiding it, but internally I’m trying. I’ve sat down, I’ve opened the document, I’ve got the pen in my hand, and somehow my attention just… slides off it. So yes, if it’s interesting, it’s much easier, but if it isn’t, even when it matters, staying focused can feel weirdly hard.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

If something genuinely catches my interest, I can stay with it much more easily, sometimes to the point where I lose track of time a bit.\n\nBut with things that are important but not immediately engaging, it’s completely different. A big reason I came in is because I can sit down with a stack of essays or a curriculum document, fully intending to get through it, and then ten minutes later I realize I’ve just been staring at the same page. I’ll have read the same paragraph several times, but I’m thinking about three other things at once and barely taking in what’s actually in front of me.\n\nAnd it’s frustrating because from the outside it probably looks like I’m being lazy or avoiding it, but internally I’m trying. I’ve sat down, I’ve opened the document, I’ve got the pen in my hand, and somehow my attention just… slides off it. So yes, if it’s interesting, it’s much easier, but if it isn’t, even when it matters, staying focused can feel weirdly hard.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you often find your attention drifting when someone is speaking to you directly, so that you realize you didn’t take in what they just said?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 31 / secondary_school_teacher / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Listening_Difficulty\nmanifestation: \"In planning meetings or even at home with my wife, I’ll be looking right at the person and nodding, but then they ask what I think and I’ve missed the actual point of what they just said.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes. That one does happen, and it’s quite embarrassing, honestly, because it can look as if I’m not interested or I don’t care, when I really am trying to listen.

It happens in planning meetings at school, for example. I’ll be sitting there looking at the person who’s speaking, nodding along, and I think from the outside I probably look engaged. But then someone will ask, “What do you think?” or they’ll refer back to the point they’ve just made, and I suddenly realize I’ve missed the actual thing they were saying. I’ve caught the tone of it, maybe, or the general topic, but not the point. Then I have to sort of piece it together from context, which is stressful.

It happens at home with my wife as well, which feels worse in a way, because obviously I do care what she’s saying. She might be telling me something about plans or something that happened, and I’m looking right at her, nodding, and then she’ll ask me a question and I realize I haven’t properly taken it in. It’s not that I’m deliberately ignoring her. It’s like my attention has drifted off even though I’m physically there and trying to follow.

So yes, I’d say that happens fairly often. Not every single conversation, but enough that it causes problems and makes me feel pretty bad about it.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Hi, yeah—thanks for seeing me. I came in because I’m having a really hard time staying with things that I genuinely intend to do. Like, I’m a secondary school teacher, and I’ll sit down with a stack of essays to mark or some curriculum document I need to read, and I’ll be completely sure I’m about to get through it properly. I’ll make the tea, clear the desk, open the first paper, all of that.\n\nAnd then, honestly, within maybe ten minutes, I realize I’ve just been staring at the same page. My eyes have moved over it, but I haven’t actually taken in what the student wrote, or I’ve read the same paragraph three times and still couldn’t tell you what it said. Meanwhile my mind has gone off in three different directions—something about tomorrow’s lesson, some unrelated thing I need to do, some random thought—and then I sort of snap back and feel frustrated because I was technically “working,” but nothing has really happened. That’s the main reason I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Did you have this same kind of difficulty focusing when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think so. I mean, I didn’t have the words for it then, obviously, and I wasn’t a disruptive child or anything, so it sort of got framed differently. But even in primary school my reports would say things like, “bright but careless,” which I remember because my parents used to repeat it to me constantly, like it was this little phrase that explained everything.\n\nAnd there were comments about me not finishing work unless the teacher was really keeping an eye on me. I’d understand the lesson, or at least seem to, but then the actual worksheet or written task just wouldn’t get completed unless someone was practically hovering. My parents were always finding crumpled worksheets in my backpack as well—things I’d done partly, or even finished sometimes, but just forgot to turn in. They’d be all folded up at the bottom of my bag days later, and I’d feel embarrassed because I hadn’t meant to not hand them in. It wasn’t like I was refusing; it was more like it just slipped out of my awareness completely.\n\nDoctor: Do you spend a lot of time worrying that you can’t control?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not sure, actually. I mean, I do worry about things in the ordinary sense, like if I’ve missed something or if I’m behind on work, but I don’t know that I’d describe it as spending a lot of time worrying in a way I can’t control.\n\nIt’s more that I get frustrated or preoccupied when something hasn’t gone the way I meant it to, if that makes sense. But if you mean constant worry that sort of takes over and I can’t switch off from it, I’m not sure that’s really the main thing for me.\n\nDoctor: Have there been long periods when you felt persistently down or unable to enjoy things?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, no—not long periods in that sense. I mean, I have definitely had times where I’ve felt fed up or discouraged, especially when I’m not keeping up with things the way I feel I should be, but I wouldn’t say I’ve had stretches where everything feels persistently bleak or where I can’t enjoy anything at all.\n\nLike, I can still enjoy things when I actually get to them—seeing friends, watching something, having a good lesson at school, that sort of thing. My mood can dip when I feel like I’ve let things pile up, but it feels more tied to the situation rather than this continuous low mood that takes over for weeks or months. At least, that’s how I’d describe it.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble organizing tasks, like breaking work into steps or keeping track of what you need to do?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that, because it depends what counts as “organizing” in the way you mean. I mean, I do keep lists and I use a calendar, especially because teaching sort of forces you to have some structure, otherwise the day just falls apart. So from the outside I probably look reasonably organized in certain ways.\n\nBut if you mean sitting down with a bigger piece of work and naturally knowing, right, first I do this, then this, then this, and keeping all those moving parts straight… I’m not sure that comes very easily to me. I often feel like I have to externalize everything—write it down, make little sub-lists, put reminders in different places—otherwise it gets a bit vague and I lose track of what I was meant to be doing next.\n\nThough I don’t know if that’s unusual or just how everyone manages things. I’ve sort of built a lot of habits around it, so it’s hard to tell what I’m like without those systems.\n\nDoctor: Do these difficulties with focus and follow-through cause problems in more than one area of your life, not just at work?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it does, actually. I’d like to say it’s mainly a work thing because teaching is just hectic and there are so many little bits to keep track of, but it’s not only there.\n\nAt work it’s become a bit more serious than just me feeling disorganized. My department chair has put me on a support plan because of missed follow-through—things I said I’d do, forms or emails or follow-ups that didn’t happen when they were supposed to. That was pretty embarrassing, honestly, because I don’t feel like I don’t care. I do care. But things slip, or I think I’ll come back to them and then I don’t, and by the time someone asks, it looks like I’ve just ignored it.\n\nAnd then at home, my wife has started taking over a lot of the bills and reminders. Not because we agreed that was just her role or anything, but because she can’t really count on me to keep track of them consistently. I’ll mean to pay something or remember something, and then it’s either late or she’s had to remind me several times, so she’s started just doing it herself. Which makes me feel guilty, because it’s not fair on her.\n\nSo yes, it’s definitely more than one area. It’s work, but it’s also home stuff, and I think that’s part of why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act or speak before thinking, even when you mean not to?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure, actually. I wouldn’t say that’s a big thing I’ve noticed about myself. I mean, everyone says the wrong thing now and then, or jumps in a bit too quickly in a conversation, but I don’t think people would describe me as someone who’s constantly blurting things out or acting without any filter.\n\nIf anything, I probably spend quite a lot of time thinking about what I’m going to say, especially at work, because as a teacher you sort of have to be careful with your words. I can occasionally interrupt if I’m anxious to get a point in before I forget it, but I don’t know if that’s the same as acting before thinking. It doesn’t feel like I’m impulsive in that way, if that’s what you mean.\n\nSo… no, not often, at least not that I’ve noticed.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel physically restless, like it’s hard to sit still?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not in a really obvious way. I mean, I’m not someone who’s constantly pacing around or unable to stay in a chair, if that’s what you mean.\n\nI can sit through meetings, and I can sit at a desk if I need to, although I suppose I might shift around or tap a pen or change position without really noticing. But I wouldn’t say I feel driven to move all the time. It’s more that sitting still can feel a bit uncomfortable if I’m bored or waiting for something, but I’ve always assumed that’s pretty normal.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I don’t think physical restlessness is one of the main things I’d point to. At least not compared with other things.\n\nDoctor: When a task is genuinely interesting to you, is it much easier for you to stay focused on it?\n\nPatient: Yes, definitely. I mean, that’s part of what makes it confusing for me, because it’s not like I can never focus on anything. Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\n{\"key\":\"S002\",\"name\":\"Sustained_Attention_Difficulty\",\"description\":\"Often has difficulty sustaining attention in tasks or play activities.\",\"reason\":\"This is the first reply of the consultation, so it should return the chief complaint symptom code.\"}\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Err toward UNKNOWN (omit) rather than guessing.\n\nReply ONLY with valid JSON (no markdown, no extra text):\n{\n  \"confirmed\": [\"S001\", ...],\n  \"denied\": [\"S025\", ...],\n  \"reasoning\": {\n    \"S001\": \"one-sentence justification\",\n    ...\n  }\n}"
  }
]

Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings). Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nHi, yeah—thanks for seeing me. I came in because I’m having a really hard time staying with things that I genuinely intend to do. Like, I’m a secondary school teacher, and I’ll sit down with a stack of essays to mark or some curriculum document I need to read, and I’ll be completely sure I’m about to get through it properly. I’ll make the tea, clear the desk, open the first paper, all of that.\n\nAnd then, honestly, within maybe ten minutes, I realize I’ve just been staring at the same page. My eyes have moved over it, but I haven’t actually taken in what the student wrote, or I’ve read the same paragraph three times and still couldn’t tell you what it said. Meanwhile my mind has gone off in three different directions—something about tomorrow’s lesson, some unrelated thing I need to do, some random thought—and then I sort of snap back and feel frustrated because I was technically “working,” but nothing has really happened. That’s the main reason I’m here.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Err toward UNKNOWN (omit) rather than guessing.\n\nReply ONLY with valid JSON (no markdown, no extra text):\n{\n  \"confirmed\": [\"S001\", ...],\n  \"denied\": [\"S025\", ...],\n  \"reasoning\": {\n    \"S001\": \"one-sentence justification\",\n    ...\n  }\n}"
  }
]

Subtypes: Initial Insomnia, Middle Insomnia, Early Morning Awakening, Hypersomnia, Nightmare Disorder, Non-Restorative Sleep\n[S028] Psychomotor_Changes: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.  Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight. Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight. Subtypes: Depressed Mood, Tearfulness, Sadness, Emptiness, Emotional Numbness\n[S025] Loss_of_Interest: A state where interest or pleasure in previously enjoyed activities is markedly diminished.  Subtypes: Loss of Interest, Social Withdrawal, Loss of Motivation, Anhedonia, Indifference to Achievement\n[S026] Weight_Appetite_Change: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.  Subtypes: Significant Weight Loss, Weight Gain, Decrease in Appetite, Increase in Appetite\n[S027] Sleep_Disturbance: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.  Subtypes: Initial Insomnia, Middle Insomnia, Early Morning Awakening, Hypersomnia, Nightmare Disorder, Non-Restorative Sleep\n[S028] Psychomotor_Changes: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.  Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight. Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others. Err toward UNKNOWN (omit) rather than guessing.\n\nReply ONLY with valid JSON (no markdown, no extra text):\n{\n  \"confirmed\": [\"S001\", ...],\n  \"denied\": [\"S025\", ...],\n  \"reasoning\": {\n    \"S001\": \"one-sentence justification\",\n    ...\n  }\n}"
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [judge] ==========

  "denied": [
    "S016",
    "S018"

    "S016": "The patient states they are not someone who is constantly blurting things out and says this does not happen often.",

Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities. Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge. Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nYes. That one does happen, and it’s quite embarrassing, honestly, because it can look as if I’m not interested or I don’t care, when I really am trying to listen.\n\nIt happens in planning meetings at school, for example. I’ll be sitting there looking at the person who’s speaking, nodding along, and I think from the outside I probably look engaged. But then someone will ask, “What do you think?” or they’ll refer back to the point they’ve just made, and I suddenly realize I’ve missed the actual thing they were saying. I’ve caught the tone of it, maybe, or the general topic, but not the point. Then I have to sort of piece it together from context, which is stressful.\n\nIt happens at home with my wife as well, which feels worse in a way, because obviously I do care what she’s saying. She might be telling me something about plans or something that happened, and I’m looking right at her, nodding, and then she’ll ask me a question and I realize I haven’t properly taken it in. It’s not that I’m deliberately ignoring her. It’s like my attention has drifted off even though I’m physically there and trying to follow.\n\nSo yes, I’d say that happens fairly often.
